					<div id="contentSub"><div id="mw-content-subtitle"></div></div>
					
					
					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Christian bishop of Smyrna (69-155)</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Polycarp_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Polycarp (disambiguation)">Polycarp (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066479718">.mw-parser-output .infobox-subbox{padding:0;border:none;margin:-3px;width:auto;min-width:100%;font-size:100%;clear:none;float:none;background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .infobox-3cols-child{margin:auto}.mw-parser-output .infobox .navbar{font-size:100%}body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-header,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-subheader,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-above,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-title,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-image,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-full-data,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-below{text-align:center}</style><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div class="honorific-prefix" style="display:inline;font-size: 77%; font-weight: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">Saint</a></div><br /><div style="display:inline;" class="fn">Polycarp of Smyrna</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a /></a></span></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;">Martyr, Church Father<br /> and Bishop of Smyrna</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data">AD 69</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">AD 155 (aged 85-86)<br /><a href="/wiki/Smyrna" title="Smyrna">Smyrna</a>, Asia, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a><br />(modern-day <a href="/wiki/Izmir" class="mw-redirect" title="Izmir">Izmir</a>, <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a>)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>,<br /><a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_East" title="Church of the East">Church of the East</a>,<br /><a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Oriental Orthodox Church">Oriental Orthodox Church</a>,<br /><a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>,<br /> <a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran Church">Lutheran Church</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">23 February (formerly 26 January)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Wearing the <a href="/wiki/Pallium" title="Pallium">pallium</a>, holding a book representing his <i>Epistle to the Philippians</i></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Earache Sufferers</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Influences</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Rome" title="Clement of Rome">Clement of Rome</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/John_the_Apostle" title="John the Apostle">John the Apostle</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Influenced</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><span class="nowrap">Major works</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><i><a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Polycarp_to_the_Philippians" title="Epistle of Polycarp to the Philippians">Epistle of Polycarp to the Philippians</a></i></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Polycarp</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;p&#39; in &#39;pie&#39;">p</span><span title="/ɒ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;body&#39;">ɒ</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="/ɑːr/: &#39;ar&#39; in &#39;far&#39;">ɑːr</span><span title="&#39;p&#39; in &#39;pie&#39;">p</span></span>/</a></span></span>; <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span lang="el">Πολύκαρπος</span>, <i>Polýkarpos</i>; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Polycarpus</i>; AD 69 – 155) was a Christian <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> of <a href="/wiki/Smyrna" title="Smyrna">Smyrna</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Britannica_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Britannica-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> According to the <i><a href="/wiki/Martyrdom_of_Polycarp" title="Martyrdom of Polycarp">Martyrdom of Polycarp</a></i>, he died a martyr, bound and burned at the stake, then stabbed when the fire failed to consume his body.<sup id="cite_ref-Wace_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wace-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp is regarded as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> and <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Father</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches" title="Oriental Orthodox Churches">Oriental Orthodox</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglican</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheran</a> churches.
</p><p>Both <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Tertullian" title="Tertullian">Tertullian</a><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> say that Polycarp had been a disciple of <a href="/wiki/John_the_Apostle" title="John the Apostle">John the Apostle</a>, one of <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">Jesus' disciples</a>. In <i><a href="/wiki/De_Viris_Illustribus_(Jerome)" title="De Viris Illustribus (Jerome)">On Illustrious Men</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a> writes that Polycarp was a disciple of John the Apostle and that John had ordained him as a bishop of Smyrna.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp is regarded as one of three chief <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Fathers" title="Apostolic Fathers">Apostolic Fathers</a>, along with <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Rome" title="Clement of Rome">Clement of Rome</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_of_Antioch" title="Ignatius of Antioch">Ignatius of Antioch</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Surviving_writings_and_early_accounts">Surviving writings and early accounts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Polycarp&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Surviving writings and early accounts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The sole surviving work attributed to him is the <i><a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Polycarp_to_the_Philippians" title="Epistle of Polycarp to the Philippians">Epistle of Polycarp to the Philippians</a></i>, a mosaic of references to the <a href="/wiki/Septuagint" title="Septuagint">Greek Scriptures</a>, which, along with an account of <i><a href="/wiki/Martyrdom_of_Polycarp" title="Martyrdom of Polycarp">Martyrdom of Polycarp</a></i>, forms part of the collection of writings called <i><a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Fathers" title="Apostolic Fathers">Apostolic Fathers</a></i>. After the <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles" title="Acts of the Apostles">Acts of the Apostles</a>, which describes the death of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Stephen" title="Saint Stephen">Stephen</a>, the <i>Martyrdom</i> is considered one of the earliest genuine accounts of a <a href="/wiki/Christian_martyr" title="Christian martyr">Christian martyrdom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Britannica_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Britannica-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Charles E. Hill argues extensively that the teachings Irenaeus ascribes to a certain apostolic "presbyter" throughout his writings represent lost teachings of Polycarp, his teacher.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A number of scholars attribute the <a href="/wiki/Pastoral_epistles" title="Pastoral epistles">pastoral epistles</a> — the biblical books <a href="/wiki/1_Timothy" class="mw-redirect" title="1 Timothy">1 Timothy</a>, <a href="/wiki/2_Timothy" class="mw-redirect" title="2 Timothy">2 Timothy</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_to_Titus" title="Epistle to Titus">Epistle to Titus</a> — to Polycarp. Because the books contain in-text attribution to Paul, Polycarp's authorship would make those book a type of forgery known as <a href="/wiki/Pseudepigrapha" title="Pseudepigrapha">pseudepigrapha</a>. Scholars who hold the view that Polycarp is the real author include <a href="/wiki/Robert_M._Grant_(theologian)" title="Robert M. Grant (theologian)">Robert Grant</a>, <a href="/wiki/I._Howard_Marshall" title="I. Howard Marshall">I. Howard Marshall</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Hans_von_Campenhausen" title="Hans von Campenhausen">Hans von Campenhausen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Polycarp&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The chief sources of information concerning the life of Polycarp are <i>The Martyrdom of Polycarp</i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Against_Heresies_(Irenaeus)" title="Against Heresies (Irenaeus)">Adversus Haereses</a></i>, <i>The Epistle to Florinus</i>, the <a href="/wiki/Epistles_of_Ignatius" class="mw-redirect" title="Epistles of Ignatius">epistles of Ignatius</a>, and Polycarp's own letter to the Philippians. In 1999, the Harris Fragments, a collection of 3rd- to 6th-century <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a> texts that mention Polycarp, were published.<sup id="cite_ref-Hartog_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hartog-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Link_to_the_Apostles_and_Jesus">Link to the Apostles and Jesus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Polycarp&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Link to the Apostles and Jesus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3><p>
According to Irenaeus, Polycarp was a companion of <a href="/wiki/Papias_of_Hierapolis" title="Papias of Hierapolis">Papias</a>, another "hearer of John", and a correspondent of Ignatius of Antioch. Ignatius addressed a letter to him and mentions him in his <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Ephesians" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Ephesians">letters to the Ephesians</a> and to the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Magnesians" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Magnesians">Magnesians</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp's epistle to the Philippians gives us some insights to the early usage of the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> from the quotes used within his letter.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Irenaeus regarded the memory of Polycarp as a link to the apostolic past. In his letter to Florinus, a fellow student of Polycarp who had become a Roman presbyter and later lapsed into heresy, Irenaeus relates how and when he <a href="/wiki/Religious_conversion" title="Religious conversion">became a Christian</a>:<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup></p><blockquote><p>I could tell you the place where the blessed Polycarp sat to preach the <a href="/wiki/Logos_(Christianity)" title="Logos (Christianity)">Word of God</a>. It is yet present to my mind with what gravity he everywhere came in and went out; what was the sanctity of his deportment, the majesty of his countenance; and what were his holy exhortations to the people. I seem to hear him now relate how he conversed with John and many others who had seen Jesus Christ, the words he had heard from their mouths.<sup id="cite_ref-SHMI_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SHMI-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In particular, he heard the account of Polycarp's discussion with John and with others who had seen <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a>. Irenaeus reports that Polycarp was converted to Christianity by apostles, was consecrated a presbyter, and communicated with many who had seen Jesus. He writes that he had had the good fortune, when young, to know Polycarp, who was then far advanced in years.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Visit_to_Anicetus">Visit to Anicetus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Polycarp&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Visit to Anicetus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>According to Irenaeus, during the time his fellow Syrian <a href="/wiki/Pope_Anicetus" title="Pope Anicetus">Anicetus</a> was <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Rome" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Rome">Bishop of Rome</a>, Polycarp visited <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> to discuss differences in the practices of the churches of <a href="/wiki/Asia_minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia minor">Asia</a> and Rome. Irenaeus states that on certain things the two speedily came to an understanding, while as to the observance of <a href="/wiki/Easter" title="Easter">Easter</a>, each adhered to his own custom, without breaking off <a href="/wiki/Full_communion" title="Full communion">full communion</a> with the other.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp followed the Eastern practice of celebrating the feast on the <a href="/wiki/Quartodeciman" class="mw-redirect" title="Quartodeciman">14th of Nisan</a>, the day of the Jewish <a href="/wiki/Passover" title="Passover">Passover</a>, regardless of the day of the week on which it fell, while Anicetus followed the Western practice of celebrating the feast on the first Sunday following the first <a href="/wiki/Full_moon" title="Full moon">full moon</a> after the <a href="/wiki/Spring_equinox_(Northern_Hemisphere)" class="mw-redirect" title="Spring equinox (Northern Hemisphere)">spring equinox</a>. Anicetus allowed Polycarp to celebrate the <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Eucharist</a> in his own church, which was regarded by the Romans as a great honor.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Martyrdom">Martyrdom</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Polycarp&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Martyrdom">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Izmir_St_Polycarp_Church_Icon_Miraculously_Extinguishing_Smyrna_Fire.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Izmir_St_Polycarp_Church_Icon_Miraculously_Extinguishing_Smyrna_Fire.JPG/220px-Izmir_St_Polycarp_Church_Icon_Miraculously_Extinguishing_Smyrna_Fire.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="131" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Izmir_St_Polycarp_Church_Icon_Miraculously_Extinguishing_Smyrna_Fire.JPG/330px-Izmir_St_Polycarp_Church_Icon_Miraculously_Extinguishing_Smyrna_Fire.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Izmir_St_Polycarp_Church_Icon_Miraculously_Extinguishing_Smyrna_Fire.JPG/440px-Izmir_St_Polycarp_Church_Icon_Miraculously_Extinguishing_Smyrna_Fire.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1958" data-file-height="1169" /></a><figcaption>Polycarp miraculously extinguishing the fire burning the city of <a href="/wiki/Smyrna" title="Smyrna">Smyrna</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In the <i>Martyrdom</i>, Polycarp is recorded as saying on the day of his death: "Eighty and six years I have served Him, and He has done me no wrong." This could indicate either that he was then eighty-six years old<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> or that he had lived eighty-six years after his conversion.<sup id="cite_ref-Wace_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wace-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp goes on to say: "How then can I blaspheme my King and Savior? You threaten me with a fire that burns for a season, and after a little while is quenched; but you are ignorant of the fire of <a href="/wiki/Everlasting_punishment" class="mw-redirect" title="Everlasting punishment">everlasting punishment</a> that is prepared for the wicked."<sup id="cite_ref-SHMI_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SHMI-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp was <a href="/wiki/Burned_at_the_stake" class="mw-redirect" title="Burned at the stake">burned at the stake</a> and pierced with a spear for refusing to burn incense to the <a href="/wiki/Roman_emperor" title="Roman emperor">Roman emperor</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> On his farewell, he said: "I bless you, Father, for judging me worthy of this hour, so that in the company of the martyrs I may share the cup of Christ."<sup id="cite_ref-SHMI_13-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SHMI-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The date of Polycarp's death is in dispute. <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Caesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Eusebius of Caesarea">Eusebius</a> dates it to the reign of <a href="/wiki/Marcus_Aurelius" title="Marcus Aurelius">Marcus Aurelius</a>, c. 166–167. However, a post-Eusebian addition to the <i>Martyrdom of Polycarp</i>, dates his death to Saturday, 23 February, in the <a href="/wiki/Proconsul" title="Proconsul">proconsulship</a> of <a href="/wiki/Lucius_Statius_Quadratus" title="Lucius Statius Quadratus">Lucius Statius Quadratus</a>, c. 155 or 156. These earlier dates better fit the tradition of his association with Ignatius and John the Evangelist.
</p><p>The <i>Martyrdom of Polycarp</i> states that Polycarp was taken on the Sabbath and killed on "the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Saturday" title="Holy Saturday">Great Sabbath</a>". English patristic scholar <a href="/wiki/William_Cave" title="William Cave">William Cave</a> (1637–1713) believed that this was evidence that the Smyrnaeans under Polycarp observed the seventh-day Sabbath, i.e. assembled on <a href="/wiki/Saturday" title="Saturday">Saturdays</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/J._B._Lightfoot" title="J. B. Lightfoot">J. B. Lightfoot</a> records as a common interpretation of the expression "the Great Sabbath" to refer to <a href="/wiki/Passover" title="Passover">Pesach</a> or another Jewish festival.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> This is contradicted by the <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_calendar" title="Hebrew calendar">standard Jewish calendar</a>, under which Nisan 14, the date of the Pesach, can fall no earlier than late March and hence at least a month after the traditional date of Polycarp's death 23 February. Hence, Lightfoot understood the expression in reference to the <a href="/wiki/Purim" title="Purim">Purim</a> festival, celebrated a month before Pesach,<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> while other scholars suggest that at the time the Jewish calendar had not yet been standardized, and that this day, both Jews and Christians celebrated <a href="/wiki/Passover" title="Passover">Pesach</a> and a (<a href="/wiki/Quartodeciman" class="mw-redirect" title="Quartodeciman">Quartodeciman</a>) <a href="/wiki/Christian_observance_of_Passover" title="Christian observance of Passover">Christian Passover</a>, respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Importance">Importance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Polycarp&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Importance">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Burghers_michael_saintpolycarp.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Burghers_michael_saintpolycarp.jpg/220px-Burghers_michael_saintpolycarp.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="291" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/21/Burghers_michael_saintpolycarp.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="300" data-file-height="397" /></a><figcaption>Engraving by Michael Burghers, ca 1685</figcaption></figure>
<p>Polycarp occupies an important place in the history of the early Christian Church,<sup id="cite_ref-Hartog_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hartog-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> was called "the most admirable Polycarp one of these [elect], in whose times among us he showed himself an apostolic and prophetic teacher and bishop of the Catholic Church in Smyrna"<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> by his contemporaries. He is among the earliest Christians whose writings survived. Jerome wrote that Polycarp was a "disciple of the apostle John and by him ordained presbyter of Smyrna".<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> He was an elder of an important congregation that was a large contributor to the founding of the Christian Church. He is from an era whose orthodoxy is widely accepted by Eastern Orthodox Churches, Oriental Orthodox Churches, <a href="/wiki/Adventism" title="Adventism">Church of God</a> groups, <a href="/wiki/Seventh-day_Sabbatarianism" class="mw-redirect" title="Seventh-day Sabbatarianism">Sabbatarian</a> groups, mainstream Protestants and Catholics alike.
</p><p>According to Eusebius, <a href="/wiki/Polycrates_of_Ephesus" title="Polycrates of Ephesus">Polycrates of Ephesus</a> cited the example of Polycarp in defense of local practices during the <a href="/wiki/Quartodecimanism" title="Quartodecimanism">quartodeciman controversy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Irenaeus, who as a young man had heard Polycarp preach, described him as<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> "a man who was of much greater weight, and a more steadfast witness of truth, than <a href="/wiki/Valentinius" class="mw-redirect" title="Valentinius">Valentinus</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Marcion" class="mw-redirect" title="Marcion">Marcion</a>, and the rest of the <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heretics</a>.” Polycarp lived in an age after the deaths of the apostles, when a variety of interpretations of the sayings of Jesus were being preached. His role was to authenticate orthodox teachings through his connection with the apostle John: "a high value was attached to the witness Polycarp could give as to the genuine tradition of old apostolic doctrine"<sup id="cite_ref-Wace_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wace-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> "his testimony condemning as offensive novelties the figments of the heretical teachers". Irenaeus states (iii. 3) that on Polycarp's visit to Rome, his testimony converted many disciples of Marcion and Valentinus.
</p><p>Polycarp is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Lesser_Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Lesser Festival (Anglicanism)">Lesser Festival</a> on <a href="/wiki/February_23" title="February 23">23 February</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> In the church <a href="/wiki/Sant%27Ambrogio_della_Massima" title="Sant&#39;Ambrogio della Massima">Sant' Ambrogio della Massima</a> in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>, Italy, there are guarded relics of Polycarp.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology">Theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Polycarp&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Polycarp._Engraving._Wellcome_V0032929.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Saint_Polycarp._Engraving._Wellcome_V0032929.jpg/220px-Saint_Polycarp._Engraving._Wellcome_V0032929.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="272" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Saint_Polycarp._Engraving._Wellcome_V0032929.jpg/330px-Saint_Polycarp._Engraving._Wellcome_V0032929.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Saint_Polycarp._Engraving._Wellcome_V0032929.jpg/440px-Saint_Polycarp._Engraving._Wellcome_V0032929.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2385" data-file-height="2953" /></a><figcaption>Saint Polycarp</figcaption></figure>
<p>Polycarp's <a href="/wiki/Soteriology" title="Soteriology">soteriology</a> is not clear; he does cite Ephesians 2:8 to say <a href="/wiki/Salvation_in_Christianity" title="Salvation in Christianity">salvation</a> is by <a href="/wiki/Grace_in_Christianity" title="Grace in Christianity">grace</a> rather than works, though later exhorts his readers to do good works. It is not clear from the text how he views works in relation to salvation as his comments are too little to make a clear conclusion. He could have believed that works are mere results of saving grace or that they are necessary to keep salvation and that they have meritorious value, thus we cannot know if he was a <a href="/wiki/Monergism" title="Monergism">monergist</a> or a <a href="/wiki/Synergism" title="Synergism">synergist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Polycarp in his letter calls <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> the "son of God" and the "eternal high priest" and that "to him all heavenly and earthly things were subjected, whom every breath worships, who comes as a judge of the living and the dead". He also highlights the sinlessness of Jesus,<sup id="cite_ref-:0_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> he defended the doctrine of the <a href="/wiki/Incarnation_(Christianity)" title="Incarnation (Christianity)">Incarnation</a> and the death of Christ in the cross, and he clearly opposed <a href="/wiki/Docetism" title="Docetism">docetism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp outright denied the teachings of <a href="/wiki/Marcion_of_Sinope" title="Marcion of Sinope">Marcion</a>, claiming he was the firstborn of <a href="/wiki/Satan" title="Satan">Satan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp's statements in the <i><a href="/wiki/Martyrdom_of_Polycarp" title="Martyrdom of Polycarp">Martyrdom of Polycarp</a></i> also appear to have <a href="/wiki/Trinity" title="Trinity">trinitarian</a> theology.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>What we know of Polycarp's eschatology is largely confined to the affirmation of the <a href="/wiki/Universal_resurrection" title="Universal resurrection">resurrection of the dead</a> and Christ's <a href="/wiki/Second_Coming" title="Second Coming">second coming</a> as a judge.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_29-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp was perhaps a <a href="/wiki/Premillennialism" title="Premillennialism">premillenialist</a>; Polycarp’s student Irenaeus was a premillenialist along with Papias who was his associate, which suggests that Polycarp also held similar views.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Polycarp refers to multiple books of the New Testament as scripture, including: <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Matthew</a>, <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles" title="Acts of the Apostles">Acts</a>, <a href="/wiki/First_Epistle_of_John" title="First Epistle of John">1 John</a>, <a href="/wiki/Epistle_to_the_Philippians" title="Epistle to the Philippians">Philippians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Jude" title="Epistle of Jude">Jude</a>, <a href="/wiki/First_Epistle_of_Peter" title="First Epistle of Peter">1 Peter</a>, <a href="/wiki/First_Epistle_to_Timothy" title="First Epistle to Timothy">1 Timothy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Second_Epistle_to_Timothy" title="Second Epistle to Timothy">2 Timothy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Epistle_to_the_Romans" title="Epistle to the Romans">Romans</a> and others.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp also quotes the deuterocanonical <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Tobit" title="Book of Tobit">book of Tobit</a>, however from his manner of quotation it is not possible to know how much authority Polycarp gave to the book of Tobit.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Polycarp's letter to the Philippians only mentions presbyters and deacons, which indicates the church at Philippi was led by a plurality of "elder-bishops", which would imply a different ecclesiastical polity than what is found in <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_of_Antioch" title="Ignatius of Antioch">Ignatius of Antioch's</a> letters. Against this, according to Steinhauser, the letter does not offer concrete evidence that the Philippian church viewed presbyters and bishops as synonyms, though still admitting that the letter still raises questions about the polity of the <a href="/wiki/Early_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Early Church">early church</a>. Steinhauser hypothesized that there was possibly a temporary vacancy in the Philippian church.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Polycarp was a <a href="/wiki/Quartodecimanism" title="Quartodecimanism">Quartodeciman</a>. According to Eusebius, Polycarp claimed that he celebrated Easter on the 14th of Nisan with <a href="/wiki/John_the_Apostle" title="John the Apostle">John the Apostle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:3_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp appears to make heresy a more serious issue than immorality. Polycarp calls immoral people to repent but called the false teachers "firstborn of Satan".<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Polycarp stated, "I have served him eighty-six years and in no way has he dealt unjustly with me". Proponents of infant baptism have argued that this quote shows Polycarp being <a href="/wiki/Infant_baptism" title="Infant baptism">baptized as an infant</a>, the argument being that if Polycarp was a servant of Christ for 86 years, he would have been a servant of Christ from infancy, suggesting infant baptism. However, Credobaptists such as Schoedel William have offered a different interpretation of the words of Polycarp, stating that the quote is ambiguous as regards to baptism, and that Polycarp can be understood as meaning by paraphrasing: "I have always served Jesus and I am not going to cease even at the age of 86."<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Polycarp&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
